--- PÁGINA 366 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
2. 
TECHNOLOGICAL FUNCTIONS 
2.1. 
Overview G35 / K 45 
The G 35 / K 45 is a new ring spinning machine generation, which succeeds the G 33 / K 44.  
The main improvements:  
• improved running behaviour, 
• higher production, 
• same or better yarn quality, 
• lower energy consumption, 
• greater cop filling quantity with ring-Ø 54 mm (up to 155 g), 
 
The goal of lowering production costs per kg of yarn, has been achieved with the above-mentioned 
improvements. 
 
2.1.1. 
Spinning geometry G 35  
 
The redesigned spinning geometry differs from that of the G 33 in the following:  
• drafting system incline, 
• drafting system depth, 
• drafting system height, 
• thread guide lift, 
• thread guide eyelets-diameter, 
• balloon control ring height and diameter, 
 
 
a) The incline of the drafting system was reduced.  
 
b) The drafting system was moved further towards 
the outside, i.e. away from the machine centre.  
 
c) The entire draft system was lowered.  
 
d) The spindles were moved from the machine 
center towards the outside, even more so than the 
draft system, i.e. the distance between spindles and 
drafting system has increased.  
 
e) Spinning-out position of the thread guide is lower, 
i.e. the total thread guide lift has been reduced / the 
spinning start-up position was adjusted.  
 
f) The thread guide eyelet-Ø was changed from 
3.7mm to 1.8mm.  
 
g) The balloon control ring height / Ø was adapted to 
the new geometry.  
 
 
H. Schwippl 
 
05.12.07 
 
                                                   8/102 
 


![Diagrama/Esquema Página 366](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/AndreLeeQuezada/manual-liztex/main/liztex_english_output/images/pag_366_completa.jpg)

--- PÁGINA 367 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
The design changes as described influence the following technology parameters:  
• The wrapping of the delivery bottom roller increases (nipping point to lift-off line). Thus the fleece angle is 
greater.  
Better support for the spinning triangle  
⇒ 
⇒ 
⇒ 
⇒ 
⇒ 
⇒ 
Advantage: better running behaviour. 
• Due to the shorter thread guide total lift, the spinning distance is influenced.  
• The greater distance of the spindles to the delivery bottom roller (horizontal) gives a bigger spinning angle. 
This results in greater wrapping on the thread guide.  
Less disturbance in thread path / fewer tension peaks / less strain on the spinning triangle  
Advantage: more stable running behaviour (better yarn quality). 
• The smaller thread guide eyelet is calming the secondary balloon.  
Improves the thread visibility / less disturbance in thread path / less movement in the spinning triangle  
Advantage: better running behaviour (better yarn quality). 
• The balloon control ring height had to be adapted so that the balloon is more balanced across the entire cop 
build up. A change was necessary, in particular in the cop base.  
 
2.1.2. 
Spinning geometry K 45  
 
The K 45 basic geometry has not changed. Changes were only made in the thread guide lift and the thread guide 
eyelet diameter, as on the BCR. This is the same as for G 35.  
As the spinning triangle of the K 44 / K 45 is very small due to the technology, the spinning geometry on the 
compact spinning machine has less influence than on the conventional ring spinning machine.  
 
The new thread guide lift does not influence the K 45 geometry in the same way as the G 35. Due to the yarn 
deflection bar, which is always in a fix position, the geometry parameters above the deflection bar are always 
constant, i.e. the fleece angle and the "upper partial spinning distance" are therefore not influenced.  
 
Below the deflection bar, the spinning angle and the spinning distance vary with the new thread guide lift, as with 
the G 35. Due to the very small spinning triangle in the compact system and the consistent fleece angle, these 
changes are not so decisive, but they support the balloon stability.  
 
The exception is the 240 and 250 mm tube. Here, spinning is carried out without the yarn deflection bar. On K 44 
the thread guide total lift with the 240 and 250 mm tube is very similar to K 45, so there is no difference here.  
 
On the K 45 the start-up spinning position and the balloon control ring height and diameter were adjusted as on 
the G 35.  
 
2.1.3. 
Drafting system settings 
 
Besides the spinning geometry, adjustments were also made to the fibre guidance in the drafting system.  
The adjustment mainly concerned the selection of the cradles and their settings. There was a change in the 
cradle selection with regard to the settings for the bottom rollers, i.e. cradle 43 for instance, was assigned to the 
bottom roller distance of today's cradle 36.  
 
Internal trials on the conventional ring spinning machine, mostly with very short staple carded cotton, have shown 
that with this new setting better yarn values can be achieved than with the classical 36 setting.  
 
The so-called drafting system setting '43 over 36' has been used on the G 33 for quite some time.  
 
H. Schwippl

pair. In addition, the main draft top roller distance is slightly closer with the settings (cradle moved slightly 
forward). 
• 
This has the effect that the unguided fibre zone (NIP) becomes smaller and thus the amount of floating 
fibres is reduced.

--- PÁGINA 369 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
2.1.4. 
Thread guide  
 
The thread guide start-up spinning positions were adjusted. The start-up spinning position is set lower over all 
tube length.  
• 
Better balloon stability in cop base due to a smaller primary balloon.  
• 
The thread tension is slightly lower on G 35. With the old setting, the balloon sometimes collapsed at the 
base.  
• 
When the thread touches the tube on cop base, the start-up spinning position has to be adjusted after a 
certain running-in time.  
 
2.1.4.1. 
Thread guide lift  
 
The thread guide total lift was shortened on the G 35.  
• 
This gives the same total lift of 72 mm for the 180 to 240 mm tube length.  
• 
The 250 mm tube has a total lift of 65 mm.  
 
The thread guide total lift of the G 33 varies from 103 mm (180 mm tube) to 65 mm (250 mm tube). The final-
spinning height of the thread guides is always measured from the cylinder support – 40 mm.  
 
 
2.1.4.2. 
Thread guide setting, changes in geometry 
 
If the thread guide lift is not set exactly as described, the geometry will be influenced. For instance, with the old 
thread guide lift setting:  
• 
> spinning angle:  
more twist jamming = more ends down  
• 
< fleece angle:  
less wrapping on the delivery bottom roller = too small spinning triangle = less fiber 
support   poor yarn quality (hairiness) and running behaviour  
• 
< spinning distance:  less buffer zone in the secondary balloon = more tension peaks from the ring / traveller 
system in the spinning triangle = more ends down and poorer yarn quality  
 
 
2.1.4.3. 
Thread guide eyelets  
 
The thread guide eyelets have a smaller diameter (old 3.7 / new 1.8 mm). These are centred, like the old thread 
guide eyelets. Due to the smaller diameter, it is not possible to go through the eyelet with the pin of the centering 
gauge.  
 
Thanks to the smaller diameter of the eyelet, the thread runs more smoothly / more stably so that, amongst other 
things, the thread visibility is better, as compared to the G 33.  
 
With the smaller eyelet diameter, centering the thread guides 
becomes more difficult.  
 
The thread guide should, nevertheless, be set as far as 
possible, so that the centering gauge lies against the thread 
guide eyelet at the back (G 35), resp. the front (K 45).  
 
 
 
H. Schwippl

--- PÁGINA 370 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
2.1.5. 
Balloon Control Ring (BCR)  
 
The G 33 has 7 differing balloon control ring heights across all formats. The balloon control ring heights on G 33 
are not adapted to the tube lengths, but only to the spinning ring diameter.  
 
The balloon control ring heights on the G 35 had to be adapted. The balloon control ring height supports balloon 
stability, and thus also run-ning behaviour.  
 
The adjustment was also made with regard to the spinning tube, the largest influencing factor, i.e. the tube length 
was taken into consideration.  
 
The balloon control rings are optionally also available in a detachable version. This is particularly recommended 
for man-made fibres and sensitive fibres, whereby a traveller weight, which is approx. 1-2 ISO heavier, must be 
adapted.  
 
The balloon control ring was generally lowered by approx. 5 mm. 
 
 
2.1.5.1. 
BCR selection  
 
Basic dimension of the balloon control ring diameter with spinning ring / tube ratio of 1:5 = spinning ring diameter 
+ 3 mm.  
  
Exceptions:  
With spinning ring / tube ratio greater or smaller than 1:5, the balloon control ring is a dimension greater than the 
relevant basic selection.  
• 
i.e. the balloon control ring diameter is not, in every case, “spinning ring + 3 mm”.  
 
Example:  
With a spinning ring diameter of 40 mm and a tube length of 210 mm (ratio 1:5.25), the balloon control ring height 
now depends on the tube length, and the balloon control ring diameter is not selected at 43 mm, but 45 mm.  
 
No special balloon control ring versions are produced for the removable balloon control ring (max. + 3 mm higher 
with the top adapter).  
 
The balloon control rings have to be assembled and centered as usual.  
 
 
 
 
2.1.6. 
Spindle speed  
 
For the new G 35 control system, the speed curve was re-worked. The aim was to make the panel more user-
friendly and logical. With the G 33 (V5-V7), there were still deviations in the input values of the spindle speed 
curve points and the graphic presentation. These deviations have been eliminated with the G 35.  
 
 
 
 
 
H. Schwippl

--- PÁGINA 371 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
The points of reference (lower turning point „ltp“ / upper turning point „utp“) had to be adapted. The different 
points were also renamed.  
In addition, two new points of reference were added in the cop base.  
 
Graphic presentation of the G 33 spindle speed curve compared to the G 35 spindle speed curve. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Spinning trials have shown that the G 35 permits a higher spindle speed than the G 33, without losses in yarn 
quality, resp. running behaviour. 
 
First of all, the spinning rings should be run in accordance with the running-in program (REP). After this, the 
spindle speed can be increased gradually. For a true comparison, the ends down and yarn quality of the starting 
situation (maximum spindle speed according to REP) should be compared with the values of the increased 
spindle speed. For the yarn quality comparison, always use the same roving bobbins, the same spinning posi-
tions, etc.. 1'000 m of yarn from the same cop height and diameter need to be tested to really be able to 
compare.  
 
The ring traveller weight is basically that of the G 33, resp. the Rieter-Grishin formula. It may need adjusting, 
depending on the speed increase.  
 
• 
If the balloon collapses at the bottom, this can be remedied as follows:  
Increase the traveller weight if the thread tension over the entire cop construction is too low.  
⇒ 
⇒ 
⇒ 
⇒ 
 
Set the spindle speed / speed curve points in the cop base higher, if the traveller weight is 
satisfactory in the cylindrical part. The new speed curve allows individual settings.  
 
• 
If the thread tension in the cylindrical part is too low / too high, this can be remedied as follows:  
Increase / reduce the traveller weight, if the balloon build-up is also poor in the cop base.  
Increase / reduce the maximum spindle speed, resp. maximum spindle speed from the ring rail 
position, if the traveller weight cannot / should not be altered at the base.

2.2. 
Bobbin changing program 
 
The SERVOgrip system makes it possible to automatically change bobbins and minimize the problems 
associated with automatic doffing and start up. 
 
 There are no yarn underwindings on the spindle, 
 Machine contamination is minimized. There should be no uncontrolled yarn remaining after the bobbins have 
been doffed. The yarn break should occur close to the yarn clamp.

All spindle types are equipped with the SERVOgrip system (underwinding free doffing).

These high forces would create unnecessary stress on the mechanical components of the doffing system and

optimal level of yarn breaking strength.

number of spinning positions can be doffed automatically without problems.  
By reducing the yarn twist during the backwinding process, the separation point in the yarn can be better defined. 
The cone winder can find the yarn end at a higher rate of success and therefore, function at a higher operating 
efficiency.

--- PÁGINA 376 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
3. 
MACHINE ELEMENTS 
3.1. 
Description and function of elements based on G 33 
 
3.1.1. 
Creel 
 
The creel arrangement can affect the machine performance in many ways. It may appear that the creel is simply 
a way to conveniently suspend roving bobbins over the drafting system. 
The creel is the starting point of the spinning operation. 
In order to maximize the weight of the roving in the creel, the bobbin holders and roving guides have to be 
precisely located to prevent abrasion of the roving as it moves to the drafting system. 
 
The critical points are: 
 
 The Roving must not rub or cross other roving. 
Roving creel 
 
 
 When bobbins of 165 mm diameter are to be hung in 
a creel with five rows it is important that the creel be 
loaded evenly with half full bobbins 
 The roving guide bars must be located at the correct height. If the bars  
are of differing heights, the tension in the roving varies and this can lead  
to count variation in the yarn. 
 Creel vibration is a sign that some component is out of balance. The creel  
tends to amplify vibrations from the lower part of the machine. 
 Creel vibrations accentuate the tendency of the roving to stretch in its  
path to the drafting zone. This can lead to yarn irregularities and count  
variation. 
 The bobbin holders and bobbin brakes, if used, should be clean and function  
correctly. Roving tension in the creel should be as uniform as possible. 
 
 
3.1.2. 
Roving guide – trumpet 
 
The roving guide is mounted on the roving traverse bar located behind the drafting system. The bar moves slowly 
to feed the roving over an extended area to spread the wear of the components and hence, extend the lifetime of 
the cots and aprons. 
The traverse motion should be set to cover as much of the surface as possible, but limiting the movement to 
ensure that the fibres do not get too close to edges. 
 
The trumpet surfaces must be smooth with low friction. They should not scuff the roving. 
 
The guiding width of the trumpet should be large enough to allow free passage of the roving. They should not 
create resistance or chokes that lead to: 
 increased yarn hairiness 
 stretching of the roving and subsequent yarn count variation 
 spinning ends down. 
 
H. Schwippl 
 
05.12.07 
 
                                                   18/102 
 


![Diagrama/Esquema Página 376](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/AndreLeeQuezada/manual-liztex/main/liztex_english_output/images/pag_376_completa.jpg)

--- PÁGINA 377 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
3.1.3. 
Drafting zone 
 
The components of the drafting system are shown below. 
 
 
Drafting system components 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The drafting system is fundamentally important in that it basically determines the uniformity of the yarn. The draft 
at spinning is higher than any other draft applied throughout the process and it handles the fibre mass when it is 
at its finest. This necessitates the precise control of the fibres being processed to suppress the formation of 
drafting waves. 
In roller drafting the fibres are accelerated as they pass from the control of the first pair of rollers to the second 
pair of rollers running at a higher surface speed.  
The short fibres are released from the first rollers and “float” until they are accelerated by the second rollers or 
accelerated by longer adjacent fibres already being drawn at the higher speed. 
 
The control of the short fibres is the primary challenge of the high performance drafting system and has been 
greatly improved by the development of the “Double Apron” drafting system for the main draft zone. The two 
aprons restrain the short fibres until they are close to the nip of the delivery rolls. 
 
Drafting waves appear as variations in mass and can be clearly seen in the knitted or woven fabric. They also are 
recognizable in the spectrogram as waves with an average wavelength of approximately 2 1/2 times the mean 
fibre length.  
 
The Ri-Q-Draft system with the Ri-Q-Bridge has been developed to enable the aprons to more precisely control 
the fibres and consequently enable higher drafts to be used without loosing fibre control. 
It should be emphasized that all elements of the drafting system are critical to the production of consistent high 
quality yarn. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
H. Schwippl 
 
05.12.07 
 
                                                   19/102 
 


![Diagrama/Esquema Página 377](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/AndreLeeQuezada/manual-liztex/main/liztex_english_output/images/pag_377_completa.jpg)

3.1.3.3. 
Settings of the drafting rollers 
 
 The bottom steel rollers (1, 2 and 3) are of 27 mm and 
lie with their centres in the same plane. 
 The back top roller (7) has a synthetic rubber cot of 30 
mm and is set slightly behind the back bottom roller 
with a backward offset (13) of 1.0 to 3.0 mm 
depending upon fibres. 
 The middle top roll /Cradle roller (6) is set with a slight 
backward offset (11) of 1.0 to 2.5 mm depending upon 
the fibres. 
 The front top roller (5) has a synthetic rubber cot of  
30 mm and normally has a forward offset (9) of 6.5 
mm. 
 The distance between the rear bottom roller (3) and 
the  
middle bottom roller (2) is referred to as the break 
draft  
distance (15). 
 The distance between the middle bottom roller and the  
bottom delivery roller is referred to as the main draft distance (14). 
 Measurements of the distances between the top rollers should be made when the rollers are loaded at 2.2 
bar  
pressure. 
 The normal clearance between the nose of the apron and the front top roller is 0.5 to 1.0 mm. In the event 
that 32 mm top rollers are used, the cradle should be moved back by 0.5 to 1.0 mm. 
 
3.1.3.4. 
Bottom steel roll settings 
 
The machines are pre set during manufacture according to the materials specified by the customer. 
The cradle length establishes the main draft zone distance. The break draft zone setting is dependent upon the 
fibre type and roving twist level.

--- PÁGINA 380 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
In most instances the standard roll settings are acceptable and should not be changed without consultation with 
the business unit. 
 
However, in some synthetic fibre spinning plants the materials being processed may contain a large portion of 
fibres exceeding the specified length for the cradle in use, i.e. fibres longer than 40mm with the 36 mm cradle, or 
fibres longer than 50 mm with the 43 mm cradle.  Under these circumstances the middle bottom roll can be 
moved backwards up to 8mm to accommodate the increased staple length. Then, the middle top rolls, with the 
cradles, and the back rollers have to be adjusted accordingly. 
 
3.1.3.5. 
Draft range 
 
With the Ri-Q-Draft double apron drafting system it is possible to achieve optimal yarn quality results with high 
drafts. There is a wide draft range potential depending upon the material and the preparation prior to spinning. 
 
Additionally it is necessary to consider the spinning ends down level as part of the optimization process. 
In practice the reality of the yarn count and the size of the available roving establishes the draft range. For 
example, the count limit of short cotton maybe Ne 30/1 and a practical roving size could be Ne 0.75/1. As a result 
the draft at spinning would be 40, which is well below the mechanical limits.

Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
3.1.3.8. 
Break draft 
 
Break draft at the spinning frame has the primary function of pre-tensioning the roving so that a consistent form of 
roving is presented to the main draft zone. 
 
 The forces involved in the break draft zone increase with the level of break draft. If the break draft is 
increased it can overload the drive systems on long machines processing fibres with high draft resistance. 
 The drafting forces also increase when the yarn count is coarser. 
 If the break draft is incrementally increased the drafting force will increase and remain steady until a “critical 
draft” is reached at which stage the drafting forces become erratic and drafting is uneven. The break draft 
should always be more than 10% below the critical level. 
 The influence of the break draft on yarn quality is relatively small, but can have a negative effect if the roll 
settings are not appropriate. 
 
 
A General Rule Is; 
 
• 
As the break draft is increased the break draft zone distance should be reduced to maintain yarn quality. 
The adverse effect is that the drafting forces increase considerably. 
⇒ 
⇒ 
 
• 
With lower break drafts the roll settings can be more open. 
The drafting forces are reduced, 
 
 With larger break draft zone distances, the system is less sensitive to variations of fibre length, roving draft 
resistance and climatic conditions. 
 Lower break drafts should be retained as long as possible.  
 
 
NOTE: Rieter always calculates the break draft using the diameter of the middle steel roller and does not 
include the apron thickness. 
In the event that a customer considers Rieter’s break draft to be low, it may be because they are basing their 
calculation on the effective diameter including the apron thickness. 
 
The apron thickness increases the effective steel roller radius by approximately 1 mm, which means that 
calculated draft would be increased accordingly.  
 
In reality, the speed of the apron is slightly lower than the speed of the steel roller because of apron slip. This 
means that the actual break draft is slightly lower than the calculated value.  
 
This phenomenon should be minimized as much as possible by the correct selection of aprons and a thorough 
maintenance program. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
H. Schwippl 
 
05.12.07 
 
                                                   24/102

In some instances it is beneficial to lower the break draft to reduce the thick and thin places that show up in

3.1.3.10. 
Cradle apron spacers 
 
The cradle is supplied with a series of spacers of different thickness that snap onto the cradle holder. The 
spacers locate the nose of the top apron relative to the nose of the bottom apron. 
 
The smaller the spacer size, the closer the top and bottom aprons. To optimally control the fibres during drafting 
the spacers have to be selected based upon yarn quality and spinning performance. 
 
 
Key: 
A = cradle length 
1  = Spacer      2  = Spacer thickness in mm 
Up to a point, thin spacers usually produce yarn of better 
IPI and evenness values. However, if the spacers are too 
thin the restraining forces will be too strong and over 
control the fibres. 
 
Over control of fibres results in: 
 
 Ends down due to ”hard ends”, 
 High thick and thin places, 
 Increased susceptibility to variation in fibre length, 
drafting cohesion and climatic conditions. 
 
H. Schwippl 
 
05.12.07 
 
                                                   25/102

--- PÁGINA 384 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
For the practical, best performance it is suggested that the preferred spacer should be 0.5 mm thicker than the 
one giving the lowest yarn CV%. This action may cause a slight increase in the yarn CV%, but there will be a 
better overall running condition. 
 
The following chart can be used as a guideline for spacer selection, but optimization is necessary. 
 
Spacer Thickness Depending upon Yarn Count 
Step-Bridge 
 
 
Ri-Q-Bridge 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Key:

о = Synthetic fibres 
 
2 = Improved running conditions 
 
X  = Cotton carded 
 
3 = Improved CV% and IPI values  
w = Cotton combed. 
In the case of man-made fibres and carded cotton, a ¼ larger distance piece is recommended. 
 
The factors that influence the effect of the aprons on the fibres being drafted are: 
 Yarn count, - finer yarns need smaller spacers, 
 Fibre length, - longer fibres need larger spacers 
 Fibre cohesiveness, - Combed cotton needs slightly smaller spacers than carded cotton even though the 
fibres of combed cotton are normally longer. Some synthetic fibres may need larger spacers. 
 
 
 
 
Key: 
4 = Distributor, rear. 
5 = Distributor front
3.1.3.11. 
Drafting guide arm 
 
The guide arm is the same for the three cradle lengths 
(36, 43 and 59 mm). The forward offset of the top delivery  
rolls is normally 6.5 mm. 
 
 
To obtain similar pressure conditions with the three 
cradles lengths, the pins (A and B) must be correctly 
located. 
 
Prior to machine shipment the pins are inserted in the 
appropriate holes for the specified cradle.

(example G33).

The pins go in holes A1 and B1 
 
 
 
 
36 mm cradle when the break draft distance is 75 mm 
If the break draft distance is increased to 75 mm, the pin

             for synthetic fibres of 50 / 51 mm. 
 
The front distributor has to be moved back one hole to 
position (B2/1). The resulting forward offset is 2.5 mm. 
 
 
 
59 mm cradle with straight guide plate. 
 
 
 
 
3.1.3.12. 
Guide arm loading 
 
The guide arms are loaded pneumatically. For fine roving or roving with low twist the pressure to the guide arm 
can be 2.1 bar. For coarse roving, or for roving with high twist or with of fibres with high draft resistance the 
pressure is 2.3 bar. If absolutely necessary the pressure can be increased slightly to 2.5 bar but component wear 
and power usage increases. 
 
NOTE: - In addition to the normal guide arm loading, a pressure-reducing valve is provided to function 
automatically when the machine is standing for a pre-selected time. This reduced pressure ensures that the yarn 
ends are secured, but that the top rolls are not deformed during standstill. 
 
 
 
H. Schwippl 
 
05.12.07

Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
3.1.4. 
Top roll cots 
 
The type and condition of the top roll cots is extremely important to the spinning plant. 
In most cases, the cots on the feed rolls are of a relatively hard type of rubber where as, the cots on the delivery 
rolls are somewhat softer. 
 
The following observations can be made: 
 The condition of the cots greatly affects the quality of the yarn, 
 The surface of the cots must be able to securely grip the fibres being drafted.  
 The cots should be buffed (ground) when signs of wear appear. Normally the time between buffing varies 
between 1,000 and 2,000 hours of operation 
 Smooth or greasy cots do not grip the fibres and cause a deterioration of yarn quality. 
 Softer cots on the delivery rolls result in better fibre control during drafting, but the softer rolls tend to lap 
when spinning blends or synthetic fibres. 
 Recommendations:  
 
Raw material 
Feed roll cots 
Shore hardness 
Delivery roll cots 
Shore hardness 
Cotton 
75 – 80° 
63 – 75° 
Man-made fibres 
65 – 75° 
75 – 85° 
Blends 
The cot should be selected on the basis of good quality and minimal lapping tendency. 
 
 
New features 32 mm cot / minimum diameter 30 mm 
 
Since the beginning of 2004 the ring spinning machines are specified and equipped with top roll cots of 32 mm 
diameter. For quality reasons the minimum diameter of these cots is limited to no less then 30 mm. This is 
especially important for the delivery cots, where good fibre nipping is very important.  
With the K44, the nip roller can be ground down to a diameter of 28 mm as previously, as long as this roller is not 
switched with the delivery cot. 
 
 
Ri-Q-Cots 
Rieter has offered its own top roller cots since January 2005. These coverings are named Ri-Q-Cot and are 
specified as first choice on machine components, unless requested differently by customers.  
As of early 2006, the assortment changed to version 2.  
The cot qualities (shore hardness) are used according to present assignment or application. 
- If possible, the top rollers should be buffed befor machine commissioning.  
 
The following top roller coverings are available: 
Designation  
Cover Quality  
Colour  
Remarks

3.1.4.3. 
New drafting system load 
 
With the larger top roll diameter of 32 mm, an improved flexing action and surface arae between top- and bottom 
rolls, is adchieved. This results in better fibre nipping and allows a slightly reduced drafting system load. 
 
In most instances the lower value of the setting rage can be selected. 
New: 
G33  
2.1 bar (range 2.1 to 2.3 bar) 
 
K44 
2.4 bar (range 2.4 to 2.7 bar) 
 
 
Despite the reduced drafting system load, it is advisable to open the guide arms, when the machine is not in 
operatation for longer periods of time. This way the top rollers are less sensitive to deformations.  
 
Deformed delivery top rollers show a peak in the spectrogram with a wave length λ of 10.5 cm (λ = top roller 
diameter (Ø 32 mm) x Pi) and are easy to differentiate from peaks of bottom rollers.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
H. Schwippl 
 
05.12.07 
 
                                                   32/102

--- PÁGINA 391 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
3.1.4.4. 
Cot buffing intervals 
 
 New cots must be buffed after an initial period of between 500 and 1,000 running hours. 
 The delivery roll cots should be buffed when signs of wear appear which is depends upon the hardness of 
the cot. Normal intervals between buffing are: 
 
Shore hardness Buffing interval 
60 – 70° 
after 1’000 – 1’500 running hours 
71 – 75 
after 1’500 – 2’000 running hours 
75 - 85 
after 2’000 – 2’500 running hours. 
 
 
 When the buffing interval is less than 2,000 hours, the diameter of the cot should be ground down by 0.2mm. 
 When the buffing interval exceeds 2,000 hours, the cot diameter should be ground down by 0.3 mm to 
ensure that the “glassy layer” has been removed completely. 
 The feed roll buffing interval can be up to four times longer than that of the delivery roll cots. 
 The Cots on the cradle rolls must never be buffed. 
 
 
With the new top roll cots 32 mm it is necessary to remove at least 0.3 mm in diameter with each buffing cycle. If 
this amount is not adhered to, inadequate quality can occur over the service live of the cot. It is also 
recommended, that new cots are buffed (levelled) before the first time use. 
The grinding instruction of ATPG, advises a surface roughness of 0.8 – 1.0 µm, which should be checked 
regularly with a calibrated measuring instrument. 
 
 
 
3.1.5. 
Aprons 
 
The control of the aprons is critical for good drafting. 
 The surfaces of the aprons have to be clean and allow the fibres to draft consistently. If the aprons become 
contaminated they should be removed and cleaned. 
 The aprons must be of the appropriate length so as to be at the correct operating tension. They should not 
be loose and buckle, nor should they be overly tight and difficult to turn. 
 The bottom apron-tensioning device should be smooth and offer the minimum amount of resistance to the 
apron movement. 
 
 
3.1.5.1. 
Top Aprons 
 
The commonly used top aprons have the following specifications:

3.1.5.2. 
Bottom Aprons 
 
For cotton and blends of cotton, most commonly leather aprons are used to achieve higher yarn quality. 
Coated leather aprons, type Torna Grey, are supplied by Sueddeutsche Textilleder (STL).  
 
The rubber apron has a superior wear cycles with yarns from synthetic fibres 
 
 
Yamauchi 
 
During the course of 2006, an increasing number of problems occurred with the Yamauchi bottom aprons.  
• 
Drawing-in of the bottom aprons  
• 
Breakages (ripping)  
• 
Detachment (peeling off) of the surface, etc.  
 
This is in particular true with bottom aprons of 1.1mm wall thickness. As a result, a change to wall thickness 
0.9mm was made in 2006.  
 
 
Daytex /Accotex 
 
In case of the rubber bottom aprons from Daytex and Accotex, the wall thickness of 0.9 mm is meanwhile 
standard. Since introduction, these aprons have proven themselves.  
As of mid-2007, Daytex will no longer offer any products for the staple fibre segment. (Filament segment only)  
 
Accotex products are solely delivered for the staple fibre segment, i.e. in future, Accotex aprons will be allocated 
for Daytex aprons. Since the introduction of the 0.9 mm bottom aprons from Accotex and Daytex, there has been 
only a minimal colour difference between the products. The actual apron characteristics are absolutely identical.  
 
 
 
Damaged aprons should be replaced immediately to prevent the production of sub-quality yarn.

Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
3.1.6. 
Twisting zone 
 
The twisting and winding zones are common, in that the rotating bobbin inserts the twist and the traveller 
simultaneously winds the yarn onto the bobbin. For practical purposes, one rotation of the traveler inserts one 
turn of twist. 
 
The twist generated in the balloon passes through the pigtail up to the delivery rollers. The twist travels to the 
point of the “spinning triangle” where the fibres emerge from the delivery rolls. 
 
The width of the spinning triangle is established by the width of the fibre strand leaving the rolls. The distance to 
the apex of the spinning triangle is influenced by the spinning geometry and the twist level.  
 
 
3.1.6.1. 
Spinning balloon 
 
The spinning balloon is generated by the centrifugal forces 
on the yarn as it rotates. The factors influencing the balloon 
shape include:  
 
 The spindle/ bobbin speed:   
Higher spindle speeds increase the forces in the 
balloon. 
 
 The length of the balloon:  
As the ring rail moves up and down the bobbin the 
balloon height and form change. 
 
 The yarn count:  
The yarn tensions increase with coarser yarns and 
the travellers have to be changed accordingly 
 
 the traveller:  
The weight of the traveller has to selected to balance 
the balloon forces and stabilize the balloon form. 
 
 
 
3.1.7. 
Ring and traveller systems 
 
Historically there have been two types of ring, the horizontal (T-flange) ring for short staple spinning and the 
vertical ring for spinning and twisting long staple, coarse counts, The vertical rings were frequently lubricated to 
enable the heavy travellers to run at higher speeds without “burning out”.  
Over the years the forms of the rings have evolved into those of our present types. 
 
 
 
 
H. Schwippl 
 
05.12.07 
 
                                                   37/102 
 


![Diagrama/Esquema Página 395](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/AndreLeeQuezada/manual-liztex/main/liztex_english_output/images/pag_395_completa.jpg)

--- PÁGINA 396 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
3.1.7.1. 
Ring classification 
 
The following is an overview of the ring types 
 
 The “T - flange normal” ring is available as the 
symmetrical form. 
 
 The “T - flange antiwedge” ring has an a-
symmetrical form. This modification was made to 
allow more clearance for the yarn between the 
upper surface of the ring and the traveller. 
 The form of the antiwedge ring reduces the ring / 
traveller area of contact and can consequently limit 
the maximum traveller speed. 
 
 The “S – flange” (inclined) ring which has been 
developed to increase the contact area between 
the ring and traveller and to reduce the pressure. 
For some yarns higher spinning speeds are 
attainable with the S – flange ring. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The T – flange rings are designated by the overall width of the flange.  
 Flange 1 – 3.2 mm 
 Flange 2 – 4.0 mm  
 The exception is the Titan AW98 ring that is only 3.2 mm wide. 
 
 
The S – flange rings are 2.8 mm wide and have the following potential advantages: 
 better heat transfer from the traveller due to the increased contact surface area, which is 4 to 5 times greater 
than the T–Flange ring. 
 Simplified running–in period due to lower traveller pressure against the ring surface. 
 Spindle speed can be increased significantly if the yarn can withstand the forces. (Warp yarn up to 25 % 
spindle speed increase) 
 Reduced thermal damage to sensitive synthetic fibres. 
 Improved running stability. 
 Fewer traveller changes. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
H. Schwippl 
 
05.12.07 
 
                                                   38/102

--- PÁGINA 398 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
The following are some of the points that have to be considered when selecting the appropriate ring diameter. 
NOTE: 
When spinning coarse yarns or yarns with low twist, the production is not limited by the spindle / traveller speed 
but by the delivery roller speed of 28.5 m/min. 
 
 Within the constraints of traveller speed, the ring diameter can be selected and the considerations are;  
 The maximum traveller speed in m/min is established by the fibres and yarn being processed. 
 The spindle speed is dependent upon the ring diameter. 
 The smaller the ring diameter, the higher can be the spindle speed to reach the maximum traveller speed. 
 The smaller the ring diameter the smaller the weight of yarn that can be wound onto the bobbin. 
 Small bobbins have to be doffed more frequently at the spinning machine. 
 Small bobbins with less yarn per bobbin require more handling and splicings per kg of yarn at the winder. 
 For optimal spinning, the length of the bobbin (tube) should be 4.7 to 5 times greater than the ring diameter. 
Consequently as the ring diameter is reduced so is the tube length that further lowers the amount of yarn 
that wound on to the bobbin. 
 Small rings are for fine yarns and large rings are for coarse yarns. 
 
3.1.7.3. 
Traveller speeds 
 
The maximum traveller speed has been increased over the years as technology and materials have been 
developed. The maximum traveller speed has to be determined by a series of tests to consider the yarn quality 
and spinning performance. This criterion is usually established before machines are specified. 
 
As a guideline: 
 When spinning cotton at 35 m/s, the traveller is considered to be running at a very high speed. 
 Manufactures of Man made fibres usually suggest that the traveller speed be limited to 28 to 30 m/s. 
 Those fibres that have a low melting point, such as polypropylene, require traveller speeds well below 28 
m/s. It is necessary to obtain the recommendations of fibre supplier. 
 
The following chart shows how the spindle speed varies in relation to the ring diameter to result in a traveller 
speed of 28 m/s. and 35 m/s

 Yarn count: coarser or finer than Ne 40/1. 
 Ring flange size: No 1 or No 2 flange (3.2 mm or 4.0 mm) 
 Ring profile: normal or antiwedge.

3.1.7.6. 
Traveller weight 
 
The weight of the traveller should be as light as possible, but the balloon must remain stable. The balloon must 
be considered at all phases of the bobbin, from the first windings at the bottom to the last windings during 
preparation for doffing. 
 
 
 
The following chart is calculated with the GRISHIN formula and is a guide to the range of traveller weights for 
different yarn counts. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The balloon must not collapse during the doffing or start-up phases of spinning. It is important to consider the 
balloon form above the balloon control ring when selecting the traveller weight. 
Traveller Weights for Cotton and Blend Yarns 
 
 
 
 
3.1.7.7. 
Estimated wear and tear of the ring and traveller 
 
The wear and tear of the traveller has to be monitored to see if the traveller is tracking correctly, otherwise 
damage of the ring could result. 
 
Visual inspection: 
 
 the travellers have to be removed with tweezers to prevent breakage. A visual inspection is necessary to see 
the wear distribution and where the traveller is making contact with the ring. 
 A sample of at least 100 travellers should be inspected to form a reliable judgment. 
 If the number of travellers judged to be badly worn exceeds 15% of the total inspected, all the travellers 
should be changed.

--- PÁGINA 404 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
3.1.8. 
Balloon control ring 
 
With relatively long bobbins and narrow gauge machines it is frequently necessary to use balloon control rings to 
limit the size of the balloon. Without the balloon control ring it would be necessary to use very heavy travellers to 
contain the balloon size, but this leads to high yarn tension and yarn breaks. 
 
The balloon control ring divides the balloon into two smaller balloons and facilitates spinning with relatively low 
tensions. 
 
Balloon Control Ring 
 
 The yarn rubs against the surface of the balloon 
control ring at speeds similar to the traveller speed. 
(30 m/s = 108 km/hr) 
 
 The balloon control ring must not interfere with the 
movement of the yarn 
 
 They should be clean and not contaminated with fibre 
finish. 
 
 They should not be damaged or scratched. 
 
 Balloon control rings can cause roughening of the 
yarn, fusing of heat sensitive fibres and fibre 
shedding. 
 
 
 
3.1.9. 
Bobbin build 
 
The vast majority of bobbins are wound with the “filling” (cop) build. The bobbin is formed in three parts: 
 the curved base, 
 the cylindrical body and  
 the conical top. 
 
The winding procedure is: 
 
 The ring rail is raised slowly to “wind” the yarn and is then moved downward at a higher speed to lay coils at 
an increased helix angle to lock-in the previous layer. 
 The upward and downward movement is referred to as the stroke. 
 The height of the stroke is normally about 15 to 20% greater than the diameter of the ring. 
 The downward movement of the ring rail is 2 to 3 times faster than the upward movement. 
 The combination of the two layers having different winding angles is beneficial in the stability of the build in 
the re-winding process. 
 Occasionally the spinning ends down will be higher during the faster downward movement and should be 
considered in setting up the speeds and conditions of the machine. 
 The stroke is short at the beginning of the build and is increased throughout the formation of the curved 
base. After the base formation the stroke length remains constant. 
 
 
 
 
H. Schwippl 
 
05.12.07 
 
                                                   46/102 
 


![Diagrama/Esquema Página 404](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/AndreLeeQuezada/manual-liztex/main/liztex_english_output/images/pag_404_completa.jpg)

--- PÁGINA 405 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
The spinning tension is greatest when the yarn is being wound directly onto the tube at the bottom of the build 
and the spindle speed has to set accordingly. Maximizing the production can be accomplished using the speed 
curve program: 
 
 During the first part of the build the spinning speed has to be established to minimize spinning breaks. 
 As the package builds and the balloon length is shortened, the spinning tension is reduced. 
 To maximize production, a control program is used to increase the spindle speed throughout the build to 
take advantage of the reduction of balloon forces. 
 The control program also slows down the spindle speed prior to stopping for the doffing cycle. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
H. Schwippl 
 
05.12.07

Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
3.2. 
K44 ComforSpin® Machine 
 
The ComforSpin® machine K 44 is an offspring of the well-proofed G 33 ring spinning machine including all of its 
advantced technology features. 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The ComforSpin® compacting unit, including: 
direct driven and wear free perforated drum 
• suction insert 
• air guide element 
• nip roller 
 
The condensing zone of the K 44 is characterized by: 
short nipping distance 
• fully controlled fiber condensing 
• high condensing efficiency 
 
The ComforSpin®  technology enables the aerodynamic parallelization and compacting of the fibers after the 
main draft. Therefore the spinning triangle is reduced to a minimum. This is the basis for the perfect yarn 
structure of the so-called fully compacted yarn – the COM4® yarn. 
 
 
H. Schwippl 
 
05.12.07 
 
                                                   48/102

Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
3.2.1. 
Compacting unit  
 
As already mentioned previously, the basis of the ComforSpin® machine K 44 is the G 33. The commonly known 
bottom delivery roller is replaced by a rotating perforated drum, with the so-called compacting unit one for each 
spinning position. 
 
Compacting zone 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The rotating perforated drums are 
available with two different designs, the 
fluted or plain surface depending on the 
application, raw material and yarn count. 
 
 
Fluted drum:      Ne 10 – Ne 100 
Plain drum:        Ne 80 – Ne 160 
 
 
 
 
air guide element
air
air
suction
air guide element
air
air
suction
 
The compacting unit consists of a rotating perforated 
drum (A) and a fixed suction insert with suction slot (B), 
which can be exchanged easily. 
 
 
 
 
 
The generated vacuum in the perforated drum causes 
the fibers to follow the slot in the suction insert to finally 
get condensed. 
 
 
 
 
This effect is intensified by the patented air guide 
element (C), which realizes mainly laterally effective air 
current.

--- PÁGINA 408 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
3.2.2. 
Nip Roller Load  
 
It was noticed, in some cases, that the nip roller load is set too high. The optimal nip roller load is around 5 to 7 
daN and is set by means of the springs in the clamping bracket, which is fixed to the guide arm.  
 
As a guideline, it can be assumed that the necessary nip roller load is achieved when the setscrew is flush with 
the outer face of the threaded hole. If the setscrews are turned in too far, it is advisable to perform some control 
measurements on the nip roller load, and if necessary, correct the screws in accordance to the above guideline.  
 
Note:  
If the nip roller load is too high, the load on the delivery top roller is reduced and consequently, the gripping of the 
fibres in the delivery roller pair is affected. In addition, this can in certain circumstances, with reduced drafting 
system pressure and despite correctly set pneumatic pressure, lead to lifting of the delivery top rollers – the fibre 
fleece can be separated and therefore a high number of ends down can occur during start-up.  
 
The nip roller load can be checked with a spring balance (clamp paper and note when the paper becomes easier 
to pull out). For this, the correct pneumatic drafting system pressure must first be set and all top rollers must be 
installed.  
 
 
 
3.2.3. 
Suction inserts K 44 / K 45 
 
A new version of suction inserts is introduced with the K 45 machine generation. The optimised airflow design will 
counteract soiling problems through improved flow conditions within the suction unit.  
With the new version, the consistency in respect to yarn quality over the entire length of the machine can be 
maintained better.  
 
 
 COM4
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
®primo  K 44 
 
K 45 
COM4®twin  K 44 
 
K 45 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The slots and slot shapes, their positions and dimensions, are precisely the same as the existing version. 
Changes were only made to the shape of the suction insert and the suction units for optimised air control.

--- PÁGINA 411 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
3.2.4.5. 
COM4®vario 
 
With the COM4®vario setup, the yarn quality such as strength and elongation, are between the conventional ring 
yarn and classical COM4® yarn. The IPI values are between or even slightly higher than conventional ring yarn. 
• A lower hairiness reduction when compared to COM4®yarn. 
• For economical reasons (yarn sales prices) the K 44 should primarily be used to produce high quality 
COM4®yarn. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
3.2.4.6. 
Pressure bar 
 
In the yarn count range Ne 10 to Ne 30 the cradle (43 mm) with pressure bar is used for carded cotton, as well as 
combed cotton with increased content of short fibres.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
If the pressure bar is removed, the cradle performs its usual function. It can therefore be used at no great 
expense with fully combed cotton, i.e. when no pressure bar is needed.  
 
 
H. Schwippl 
 
05.12.07 
 
                                                   53/102

--- PÁGINA 412 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
3.3. 
Core-Yarn application on K 44 ComforSpin® machine 
3.3.1. 
Overview 
 
Depending on the type of ring spinning machine (G or K) different requirements exist for a core yarn device. With 
the K-machines e.g. the roving guide to each individual spinning position must be adjustable to be aligned 
individually to the compacting suction-slots.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
From a technological viewpoint, cotton yarns with obviously better values could be manufactured with the core 
yarn device, when compared to normal compact yarn properties such as evenness, IPI values and elongation. 
Although a significant loss in yarn strength had to be accepted, caused by the structural change.  
 
Climatic influence 
Already at the beginning of our trials it turned out that the K 44 reacts extremely sensitively to cold air flows from 
above (air conditioning ducts), with a reference yarn of Ne 50, this is even without a core filament. 
Excessive yarn breaks and roving pull through, which were absolutely unacceptable in their high numbers, were 
the negative result.  
 
Influence of core 
Above all the evenness and to a high degree the thin places were improved by adding the filament core. With a 
coarser core, it is noticeable, that the yarn hairiness becomes obviously worse. Perhaps the filament core is 
already too large, so that the integration of the fibres in the spinning triangle is disturbed. Except in yarn hairiness, 
the yarn with the coarser core shows somewhat better values, the differences however are very small.  
 
Positioning of core 
Inadequate positioning of the core (this has to be centred in the spinning triangle) shows up only partially in the 
yarn values. The evenness and IPI values measure slightly worse, the hairiness, strength and abrasion result s 
are even better, caused by the different structural yarn covering. 
 
Covering of core 
Examination of the core covering through dying with a contrast colour was not successful (the cotton fibres dye, 
Lycra does not), since the Lycra was also accepting some staining. 
 
Thin places in knit fabric 
A significant amount of thin places are visible in the knitted fabric. The cause was found in core breaks, which 
were not from the supply side into the drafting, since the core is actively monitored. 
 
 
H. Schwippl 
 
05.12.07 
 
                                                   54/102 
 


![Diagrama/Esquema Página 412](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/AndreLeeQuezada/manual-liztex/main/liztex_english_output/images/pag_412_completa.jpg)

--- PÁGINA 413 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
Core breaks 
Core breaks are not detectable in the yarn testing process, based on the minimal mass decrease and shortness 
of length and therefore do not sway the examination results. 
 
The core breaks will show-up only in the knitted fabric, since the loops are not closing evenly due to missing 
contraction force and remain about the size of the reference fabric without Lycra. 
 
These core breaks are caused on the ring/traveller system through thermal overload and the inadequate covering 
of the core. 
 
The covering factor of the core can be positively influenced with the Linea suction insert (somewhat lower yarn 
values have to be accepted) and through higher draft application to the core. 
 
 
Traveller requirements 
By lowering the spindle speed and applying a somewhat higher bowed traveller, as well as a suitable spinning-
ring type  
(CR-Ring is better then Titan N98) the thermal overload can be reduced or even eliminated. 
 
The traveller requirements for compact core yarns are in principal contradicting. On one hand the traveller shape 
should exhibit a low profile due to low yarn hairiness with compact yarns and the need for traveller lubrication, but 
on the other hand due to danger of thermal damage to the core, should have a high bow, i.e. only a narrow path 
to find the right compromise. 
 
 
Elastic core yarns have obviously different yarn characteristics than conventional yarns. With this realisation in 
mind, standard yarn testing methods had to be modified (e.g. yarn sorting with adjustable brakes) and new ones 
compiled (evaluation covering of core). 
 
 
Sorting of core yarn 
By adding a flexible filament during the spinning 
process, the yarn exhibits substantially higher 
contraction forces than conventional yarns. 
 
 
When applying the conventional yarn sorting 
process, snarls are formed on the measuring 
reel; the yarn does not tension properly and thus 
the yarn count is measured as much too coarse. 
 
 
 
 
To prevent the build-up of snarls on the reel, 
adjustable brakes had to be developed. Since the 
contraction force of core yarns depend on several 
factors, such as yarn twist, proportion of core and 
draft of core, optimal brake adjustments have to 
be established through tests.  
 
 
 
 
H. Schwippl 
 
05.12.07 
 
                                                   55/102 
 


![Diagrama/Esquema Página 413](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/AndreLeeQuezada/manual-liztex/main/liztex_english_output/images/pag_413_completa.jpg)

--- PÁGINA 414 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
Based on a conventional yarn count of Ne 50, as a first attempt, two cores of 22 and 44 dtex were simply added 
in the spinning process and as expected, the yarn count did not yet conform with the target range. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The trials showed as expected, that with increasing brake forces the yarn count measured lighter and with the 
highest setting (8), the yarn even broke. It is not reasonable to test with such high brake forces, since the yarn is 
already over-stretched (plastic deformation) in this stage. 
With a second test series, the machine draft was adjusted until the yarn count produced corresponded with a Ne 
50. Staring from setting 1, the braking force was gradually adjusted until no snarls developed on the reel. 
The core yarn with a 22 dtex filament showed an optimal brake force setting at 1.75 and with 44 dtex at 2.25.

Roving count Nm 2.0 and Nm 3.5 of 100% combed cotton with the assortment acronym “Prim”, intended for yarn 
counts up to Nm 135.

3.3.4. 
Evaluation covering of core in knitted fabric 
 
A significant criterion with core yarns is the absolute covering of the core filament. This can be visualized through 
a contrasting dye process of the fabric, i.e. the cotton fibres accept the dye, while the core filament remains in 
natural stage. 
The knit fabric was produced on a Terrot-Knitting machine with 24-bobbin feed for each test position. The 
subsequent finishing process included; pre-wash, bleaching, dying, final wash and ended with drying /affixation. 
 
To evaluate if the dying process would reveal the coverag of the core, 2 positions were made, where the core 
alignement was not centred to the spinning triangle, but offset by 1.5 mm to the right. In this case the core is not 
fully covered and should be visible.

3.3.4.2. 
Evaluation without / with core 
 
With the integration of a core, the evenness and IPI values are distinct to very distinctively better. At the other 
hand the yarn strength suffers a significant decrease, which cannot simply be explained by the reduced number 
of fibres in the yarn cross-section. The yarn Ne 50 with the 22 dtex core (core relation = 6.7%) has only 93.3% of 
the cotton fibres available, which on a calculus base, would result in about 25.5 cN/tex based on 27.3 cN/tex 
without core.  
The core is not included in the calculation, since it has a 400 – 500% break elongation factor and does not 
contribute to the yarn strength.  
 
The reason has to be found in the structure of the 
yarn. The yarn strength is derived through the 
twist application and the significant increase in 
fibre-to-fibre friction.  
 
Since this is more pronounced in the centre of 
the yarn then the outer layer, it has to be 
assumed, that with core yarn where a core 
filament is integrated in the centre, that the 
strength is disproportionally decreased. 
 
 
H. Schwippl 
 
05.12.07 
 
                                                   59/102 
 


![Diagrama/Esquema Página 417](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/AndreLeeQuezada/manual-liztex/main/liztex_english_output/images/pag_417_completa.jpg)

--- PÁGINA 418 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
3.3.4.3. 
Impact of core titer 22/44 dtex 
 
The evenness and IPI values between these two titers result in no clear differences. The hairiness is increased 
with the coarser titer. It has to be assumed, that the core has already reached a critical size, where the integration 
of the fibres into the covering is hindered in the spinning triangle. As expected, the strength is further reduced 
with the coarser titer based on the higher core segment (10.6%) and the resulting lower cotton proportion of 
89.4%. 
 
 
3.3.4.4. 
Impact of core position 
 
The spinning triangle is distorted when the core is 
fed in an off-centred position. As a result the 
evenness and IPI values are somewhat worse. 
On the other hand, the hairiness, strength and 
shedding are deliberately improved, since the 
Lycra is wrapped more around the yarn then 
integrated into the core. 
 
 
Conclusion: A bad positioned core does not automatically result in inveriour yarn values.  
Yarn

Nipping point

Nip roller

Core centred
Yarn

Perforated drum –
Nip roller
Spinning triangle
Core centred
 
 
3.3.4.5. 
Covering of core 
 
All core yarn positions, even the once where the core was fed off-centred in relation to the spinning triangle, show 
no white spots from the Lycra in the finished and dyed knitting fabric. The reason is to be found in the fact that the 
Lycra is “stained” by the dye and therefore, no visible differences in contrast exist. 
 
The only differences exist in the colour intensity. The reference fabric without core is slightly darker, which can be 
explained by a more thorough yarn dying factor (no foreign core filament present), as well as a more open 
structure of the knitting fabric. Here, the Lycra does not tighten the loops and a larger surface area can be dyed, 
with a different light reflection. In addition, the position “Core Compact Ne 50, Pos 3” (44 dtex, core centred) with 
a higher hairiness appears also somewhat darker. In principal, with a coarse core, where the position of the 
filament influences the yarn hairiness and as a result the colour nuance, the danger exists to create a barre effect 
when not all core filament-feeders are adjusted equal. 
 
 
3.3.4.6. 
Thin places in dyed fabric 
 
When viewing the core yarn knit fabric in transparency, a 
notable amount of thin places were immediately evident. 
With the fine 22 dtex titer (fabric section of 13 x 10 cm) 
they were so numerous that they couldn’t be counted. 
 
 
 
 
 
H. Schwippl

No thin places were found in the reference yarn without core.

coarser one none at all, even though there are fewer fibres 
available for the covering. The reason can be found in the fact

![Diagrama/Esquema Página 419](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/AndreLeeQuezada/manual-liztex/main/liztex_english_output/images/pag_419_completa.jpg)

--- PÁGINA 420 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
When the coarse core is fed off-centred to the spinning triangle and as a result not completely covered, then core 
breaks and thin places will also occur here. This is valid for both core types.  
But it has to be noted, that the core with more filaments is more favourable in regard of amount and also length of 
the faults. 
 
Even with the 22 dtex position with an infinite amount of thin places in the knit fabric, no inferiour yarn values 
were measured. 
The questions arrise now, can these core breaks be captures with yarn testing methods and where are they 
triggered, the ring spinning machine or knitting process? 
 
 
3.3.5. 
Evaluation with measuring instruments, origin of core breaks 
 
 
A repeat of the spinning trial of the yarn position with the 22 dtex core was made, with follow-through of lab-
evaluation.  
The evenness diagram of the UT4 instrument should reveal the mass variations. It has to be recognized that the 
core breaks are not visualized with these standard testing methods, because of the low mass decrease (-6.7%) 
and shortness in length, mostly < 5 cm.  
 
 
 
 
Even with a significantly stretched length scale, the core breaks are not recognizable. 
In general the naturally occurring mass variations in the yarn body are larger then the once related to a core 
break. This is a reason why additional tests with UT4 optical, Zweigle optical with knit fabric simulation (Oasys) 
and winding onto yarn tables yielded no success. 
  
Conclusion: The core breaks in the yarn cannot be captured with laboratory measuring instruments. They can 
only be made visible indirectly, as a thin place in the knit fabric, due to missing retention force. 
 
 
 
 
 
H. Schwippl 
 
05.12.07 
 
                                                   62/102 
 


![Diagrama/Esquema Página 420](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/AndreLeeQuezada/manual-liztex/main/liztex_english_output/images/pag_420_completa.jpg)

--- PÁGINA 421 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
3.3.6. 
Origin of core breaks 
 
While pulling the yarn off the bobbin, it became evident, that the core breaks happened always shortly before or 
directly at the upper ring rail occilation point. A direct influence of the ring /traveller system is now certain. 
 
 
Examining the broken core ends clearly 
showed, that often a thermal overload on 
the filament and in the same area also 
frequently burnt cotton fibres existed.  
 
 
 
Conclusion: Many core breaks on the ring spinning machine are caused by thermal overload within the ring 
/traveller system. 
 
3.3.7. 
Influence on core breaks by ring spinning machine 
 
Since no core breaks took place with the coarse filament, the following trials were made exclusively with the fine 
22 dtex titer. The yarn count remained at Ne 50. Due to better visibility and therefore adjustability, a white core 
type (136 B) was added. The position of the core was always centred to the spinning triangle. 24 bobbins were 
produced with each position, whereby bobbins 1–18 were used for lab-evaluations and bobbins 19–24 to produce 
a laboratory knit-fabric. To eliminate the influence of traveller wear, a new set was used at the beginning of each 
position. 
 
The run-time for full bobbins is with 7.5 hours very long. Since only a part of the bobbin was required for the 
standard testing as well as knitting, the production time was reduced to 6 hours. 
 
3.3.7.1. 
Positions 
 
The core breaks on the ring /traveller system can be caused by an inadequate covering of the core and through 
excessive thermal load on the ring /traveller. Therefore especially those influence factors were evaluated.

--- PÁGINA 422 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
3.3.7.2. 
Yarn quality 
 
The yarn quality of the first spinning trials were in tendency slightly better, since full bobbins were produced and 
in the upper range, where the yarn testing was made, with reduced spindle speed operated. 
 
The various core types, traveller- and ring types, as well as the amount of draft of the core had no influence on 
the evenness and IPI values. An exception is the CR-ring, where the amount of neps was slightly elevated. 
 
As expected, the yarn quality turns out somewhat better with lower spindle speed. 
 
While results from previous trials without core showed no significant differences between the suction inserts, 
there was now, with core and the Linea suction insert, a clear deterioration of values, except in the amount of 
neps. Hence, the core does influence the main drafting process negatively, since the filament is added in the 
centre of the roving strand, which has to be drafted by the delivery top roller.  
This is not the case with the Primo suction insert. There, the core feeds offset to one side on the delivery top 
roller (main draft is not disturbed) and is joined with the cotton fibres only at the end of the compacting zone.

The results from the first test series are confirmed here, where a very high amount of thin places developed in the 
knit fabric with the transparent core filament (175 Clear) in conjunction with the Primo suction insert. It is also 
visible, that a large spread within the spinning positions exists. For these reasons, an additional position with a 
normal CR-ring without Titanium coating was made. 
 
 
3.3.7.4. 
Influence ring /traveller system 
 
Influence Spindle Speed 
Through the reduction in spindle speed from 12’000 → 10’500 min-1 and as a result the lower thermal load, the 
amount of thin places were reduced substantially, but not completely eliminated. 
 
Influence Taveller Type 
With the high bowed traveller shape SL dr (Yarn path far away from ring surface, reduced thermal load) no thin 
places developed. The traveller’s wire cross-section has to be taken into consideration here. The C1 EL udr is flat 
round and the C1 SL dr is half round (SL is only in dr available!) i.e. the yarn is also more gently guided. 
Additional trials with various wire cross-sections would be interesting. 
 
The traveller requirements for K 44 core yarns are in principal contradicting. On one hand the traveller 
shape should exhibit a low profile due to low yarn hairiness (deprived lubrication) with compact yarns and the 
need for traveller lubrication, but on the other hand due to danger of thermal damage to the core, should have a 
high bow. Hence, there is only a narrow path to find the right compromise. 
During a pre-trial with the traveller type C1 UL udr (shaped with even higher bow), 3 travellers burned up within a 
single bobbin-run and therefore was eliminated early on. 
 
H. Schwippl 
 
05.12.07 
 
                                                   67/102 
 


![Diagrama/Esquema Página 425](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/AndreLeeQuezada/manual-liztex/main/liztex_english_output/images/pag_425_completa.jpg)

--- PÁGINA 426 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
Influence Ring Type 
Based on the wide spread within the spinning positions a trial with a normal CR-ring without Titanium coating was 
made. Surprisingly, absolutely no thin places were visible in the knit fabric. Hence, not only the traveller itself, but 
also the spinning ring has a large influence. 
 
 
3.3.7.5. 
Influence covering of core 
 
To visualise the covering of the core more clearly, a black core filament was used. Through the difference in 
contrast of the light cotton fibres against the dark core, the covering was observed with the aid of a microscope. 
The spun yarn positions for the lab-testing and knit fabric evaluation, were all made with the normal white and 
transparent core type. 
 
Influence Draft of Core 
It was observed under the microscope, that the core with the higher draft (V = 3.3) was not as visible on the yarn 
surface, i.e. better integrated into spinning process. Perhaps the core remained better centred within the yarn due 
to the higher tension applied. 
This is also clearly evident by the number of thin places in the knit fabric, which could be almost eliminated 
completely. 
 
Influence Suction Insert Type 
It was also observed under the microscope, that the core exhibits a superior encapsulation on yarns produced 
with the Linea suction insert. As a consequence, the thin places in the knit fabric could also be significantly 
reduced. 
It has to be remarked here, that the yarn hairiness level was slightly higher, better lubrication ring /traveller 
system, and potentially acted as an influence factor.  
In general the entire yarn quality was slightly inferior with the Linea suction insert. 
 
Conclusion: 
A good encapsulation is just as important as a low thermal load on the ring /traveller system. 
The covering of the core can be observed either directly with the aid of a microscope, or indirectly through core 
breaks (by critical machine adjustment) and as a result, observed thin places in the knit fabric. 
 
 
 
3.3.7.6. 
Influence core type 
 
There are considerable differences between the two core types. An examination under the microscope revealed, 
that the white core type 136B, which resulted in no thin places in the knit fabric, actually contains 3 filaments 
(compared with 175C = Monofil) and therefore showed a significantly higher resistant against core breaks.  
 
These two core types differentiate also in the strength /elongation characteristics. The white type exhibits at 200% 
elongation a notably higher strength, which affects the core encapsulation positively, see above; Influence Draft 
of Core. 
 
 
 
 
 
H. Schwippl 
 
05.12.07 
 
                                                   68/102 
 


![Diagrama/Esquema Página 426](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/AndreLeeQuezada/manual-liztex/main/liztex_english_output/images/pag_426_completa.jpg)

--- PÁGINA 427 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
3.3.7.7. 
Comparison yarn-values / thin-places in knit fabric 
 
The test positions with a high number of thin places in the knit fabric did not exhibit inferior yarn properties, i.e. 
there exists no correlation.  
As demonstrated earlier under the subject “Evaluation with measuring instruments”, this was expected, since the 
core breaks due to their small size, are not measurable and therefore do not affect the measured yarn values. 
 
Conclusion: 
The yarn results are simply not sufficient to evaluate each individual adjustment on the ring spinning machine and 
for this reason a knit fabric sample must be produced for evaluation. 
 
 
3.3.7.8. 
Influence knitting machine 
 
Since the main evaluation takes place with the knitted fabric, the influence factors of the knitting machine should 
be known. For this reason, an additional test with higher knitting speeds was performed. Two bobbins from 
position Core Comp. Ne 50, Pos 9 were selected, where with the standard speed (position 7) already a small 
amount of thin places existed.  
 
As visualized in the following chart, the amount of short faults up to 0.5 cm, are massively increased. Hence, core 
breaks and as a result thin places in the knit fabric can also be caused on the knitting machine.  
The newly added faults are very short in length, in the range of 1-2 mm.  
The question arises here, were the core filaments already pre-damaged on the ring /traveller system?

--- PÁGINA 428 (MANUAL_INGLES) ---
 
 
Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
4. 
RECOMMENDATIONS 
4.1. 
Start-up suggestions 
 
¾ Prior to spinning it is very important that the machine be in a correct operating condition. 
¾ Check that the machine is correctly levelled. Inaccurate levelling can produce an “out of round running 
condition that frequently shows up as a 10 cm spectrogram peak in the yarn. 
¾ The rings must be correctly centred, (Very Important) preferably using the electronic indicator. The device 
should be carefully calibrated. The centring function should be performed by the erector and not left to 
assisting personnel provided by the plant. 
¾ Check to be sure that the yarn thread guides are correctly located over the tubes. The yarn balloon must be 
symmetrical around the bobbin. 
¾ Ensure that the ring rail is level over the full length of the machine, otherwise the doffing function will be 
compromised and the bobbin build will not be uniform. 
¾ Check the setting of the roving guides to be sure that the roving will pass through the drafting system in the 
central zone. 
¾ Set the draft roll distances for the fibres to be spun. In most case the rolls will have been pre – set according 
the customer’s fibre specifications. 
¾ Set the break draft and total draft according to the roving size and the required yarn count. 
¾ On the basis of the fibres to be spun and the yarn count needed, select one of the standard settings for: 
Cradles and aprons, 
Roller settings – bottom and top, 
Draft distribution. 
 
¾ Set the spindle speed, taking note of any pre-sales tests that have been done. 
¾ Program the speed curve, the bobbin build and the bobbin weight so that critical conditions are avoided at 
start up 
¾ Select the traveller type as established by any pre sales trials or use those supplied with the machine. 
¾ If necessary set the traveller clearer for the type and size of the traveller to be used. If possible avoid 
changing the clearer positions until the running conditions are established. 
¾ Follow the ring run-in program when setting up a new machine. 
¾ Pre spin on 10 to 20 positions for sizing and initial checks of balloon form and the general spinning 
conditions. 
¾ Feed the roving immediately ahead of the spinning-on step. Avoid having multiple ends of roving being 
drafted and feeding fibre to the waste collection system while other ends are being started. 
¾ At the start of spinning, clamp the seed yarn under the tube and wind approximately five wraps around the 
bobbin at a height not to exceed the strokes of the ring rail. 
¾ Avoid winding “start up yarn” above the corresponding ring rail stroke. Excessive start up yarn on the bobbin 
tends to become entangled in the yarn as it is being wound. It also creates problems of yarn dragging at the 
winding machine. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
H. Schwippl 
 
05.12.07

4.2. 
Recommendations for COM4® downstream processing 
 
COM4® as every yarn type, owns an own characteristic. This also means that there are slight differences in 
downstream processing. In the following chapter you will find some of these differences explained and how to re-
act. 
Generally spoken, COM4® is a compacted yarn. Fibers are more straightened and more paralyzed. This means 
that the diameter of the yarn body is about 2 % less (UT 4) than conventional ring yarn. Additionally the hairiness 
is strongly reduced. Tenacity and elongation are increased. 
This means gives you the following points of attention: 
 
4.2.1. 
Knitting 
 
Covering is worse 
- find different constructions using visibility of knitting construction 
 
Exchange of existing articles 
- when copying existing articles, the result with COM4® yarn may differ 
- reasons are missing hairiness and lower covering (see above) 
- create new articles for compact yarn to show the yarn characteristics to its best advantage 
- caused by the reduced hairiness, yarn tension may differ during processing 
- check and correct the adjustment of brakes 
 
Fiber fly is less 
- increase interval of cleaning cycles 
- look more for constructions using different fiber types or different colors 
Lower hairiness for better optic 
- use the potential regarding luster und brilliancy 
- find high-value articles 
- consider eliminating the singeing process 
 
Eliminate singeing 
- increase interval of cleaning cycles 
 
Higher security regarding ends-down 
- higher, uniform strength gives the opportunity to increase number of machines per worker 
- think of non supervised shifts 
 
- the same amount of dye stuff gives darker shades 
- ask for yarn with lower twist 
- decrease loop length 
 
Yarn tension 
(you will have much less troubles than with conventional ring yarn) 
 
- due to the better structure of the yarn, singeing can be eliminated in many cases 
- lower wear and tear on the knitting machine 
Color 
- COM4® colors look more cold than hairy conventional ring yarn

Technology Handbook 
 
 
 
 
4.3. 
Ring- / traveller system COM4® 
 
 
With the COM4® yarn, poor lubrication in the area of the ring-/traveller system is still the main issue in respect to 
production limits. The traveller types that can be used with COM4® yarn are very limited and must be selected 
with great care. Also, the right choice of traveller weight is crucial to whether satisfactory running behaviour can 
be expected, assuming of course that the basic conditions are also correct.  
This is also very important in order, amongst other things, to keep the loss of yarn elongation within bounds.  
 
With very fine yarns (≈ Ne 70) and/or high spindle speeds, the traveller C ½ EL udr Saphir is predominantly used. 
This type of traveller is very low-bowed (= narrow yarn opening), i.e. with very fine COM4® yarns the lubrication 
of the ring-/traveller system is clearly assisted.  
Owing to better lubrication, higher spindle speeds than with the C 1 EL udr traveller can be achieved, or articles 
can be run that would otherwise be virtually impossible (qualitatively and economically) to manufacture.  
 
To an extent, a disadvantage of this traveller is the increased ends down at cop change (severed threads at snarl 
lift) caused by the fact that the traveller has almost no degree of freedom on the spinning ring.  
 
Essentially, with high absolute air humidity, i.e. with high water content in the air, one to two ISO numbers lighter 
should be selected. The same applies when very high spindle speeds are used, i.e. above the spindle speeds 
stated in the ring run-in program.
